From all-dieletric nanoresonators to extended quasi-static plasmonic resonators Thanks: ∗ Department of Applied Mathematics, The Hong Kong Polytechnic University. Email: xinlin.cao@polyu.edu.hk; xlcao.pdeip@gmail.com. Thanks: ∗∗ Nanjing Center for Applied Mathematics, Nanjing, 211135, People’s Republic of China. Email: gh.hsen@njcam.org.cn. Thanks: ‡ RICAM, Austrian Academy of Sciences, Altenbergerstrasse 69, A-4040, Linz, Austria. Email: mourad.sini@oeaw.ac.at. This author is partially supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 32660
Xinlin Cao ∗ Ahcene Ghandriche ∗∗ and Mourad Sini ‡

Abstract
. We derive the electromagnetic medium equivalent to a cluster of all-dielectric nanoparticles (i.e. enjoying high refractive indices), distributed periodically in a smooth domain Ω \Omega , while excited at nearly resonating dielectric incident frequencies (i.e. subwavelength Mie-resonant frequencies). This effective medium is an alteration of the permeability that keeps the permittivity unchanged. We provide regimes under which the effective permeability can be positive or negative valued. In addition, if the incident frequency is close to any of the subwavelength all-dielectric resonances, then the distributed cluster behaves as an extended quasi-static plasmonic resonator. Therefore, exciting the cluster of all-dielectric nanoresonators with nearly resonating incident frequencies, we can generate an extended quasi-static plasmonic resonator which creates giant electromagnetic fields in its surrounding. Keywords : Maxwell system, dielectric nanoparticles, negative media, plasmonic resonances, Mie resonances, effective medium theory. AMS subject classification : 35C15; 35C20; 35Q60



1.
Introduction and statement of the results

1.1.
Background and motivation

The nanoscale optics structures are usually
manufactured using building-blocks made of plasmonics, with
metals such as gold or silver, enjoying negative real part of the permittivity, or dielectrics, as silicon. Each type of material (metal or dielectric) has advantages and disadvantages, see
[
37
,
27
]
for related discussions. Such properties are due mainly to their resonant effects. Indeed, both material can resonate at specific incident frequencies. The metals are known to resonate at the so-called plasmonic resonances which are related to the eigenvalues of the Magnetization operator while the dielectrics resonate at the so-called all-dielectric resonances (or subwavelenght Mie-resonances) which are related to the vector-Newtonian operator. A natural way of representing the electromagnetic field generated by such structures is the Lippmann-Schwinger system of equations, via the Maxwell dyadic fundamental tensor. This integral operator is given as a sum of the two operators we just mentioned, namely the Magnetization and vector-Newtonian operators. Such a point of view provides a unified way for studying the resonant effects generated by the types of contrast of the permittivity/permeability of the structure as compared to the surrounding background. This approach was used, with eventually different strategies and applications, to study the subwavelenght plasmonic resonances in
[
24
]
and dielectric resonances in
[
4
,
15
]
while created by a single nanoparticle and in
[
6
,
13
,
16
,
5
]
while created by a dense cluster of such resonators. Let us mention a notable difference between the field created by plasmonic resonators and the ones created by dielectric ones when the contrast appears in the electric permittivity (considering non-magnetic materials). In the former case, only the electric permittivity is averaged and changed while the permeability is kept the same. In the latter case, however, the the opposite holds, namely the permittivity is unchanged while the effective permeability is changed. This last phenomenon is quite surprising as it means that we can generate purely magnetic materials using a nanostructure of purely electric (i.e. non-magnetic) building blocks. This last observation is already made in the engineering literature for spherically shaped nanoparticles using formally the classical Mie-computations,
[
34
]
, see also
[
27
]
for a review on the related applied liteature. In addition, it was justified using the homogenization techniques in
[
11
]
assuming the nanoparticles to be absorbing (i.e. with a non-vanishing imaginary part of the permittivity) and tacking the incident frequencies away from the dielectric resonant frequencies (or the subwavelenght Mie resonant frequencies). In the current work, we go one step forward by taking incident frequencies close to these resonant frequencies, regardless of the absorption assumption, and characterize the equivalent medium.
The outcome can be summarized as follows

(1)

As we allow incident frequencies to be close to the resonant frequencies then the volumetric concentration of the nano-particles can be low. Such a property might be very useful in practice as we do not need to (or we cannot) inject highly dense clusters of nano-particles to get a desired effective field. This can be seen in Assumptions (
1.13
) and (
1.14
) of
Theorem
1.4
which means that the number of nano-particles is of the order
a^{h-3}
with
h
close to
1
while the usual homogenization, with frequencies away from the resonant frequencies, need to take this number of the order
a^{-3}
where
a
,
a\ll 1
, models the maximum radius of the nano-particles.

(2)

The effective permeability is changed, in contrast to the permittivity, and it can be positive or negative signed depending on the choice of the incident frequency. Therefore, the equivalent and extended medium can behave as a plasmon or as dielectric again depending on the choice of the incident frequency. This is described in
Theorem
1.4
and discussed more in
Remark
1.5
.

(3)

We show that the equivalent extended medium can generate low frequency plasmonic resonances. In other words, this result means that we can generate extended quasi-static plasmonic resonators from structures of all-dielectric nanoresonators. This is described in
Theorem
1.6
and discussed more in
Remark
1.7
and
Remark
1.8
.

An important consequence of these results is that we can use all-dielectric nanoparticles, with well tuned optical properties, to generate high and even giant electromagnetic fields, see the discussions in
Remark
1.7
and
Remark
1.8
for more details. In addition to its mathematical interest, the
generation of high or eventually giant electromagnetic fields by designs coming from nanostructures is highly attractive and desirable, see the discussions in
[
29
]
and the references therein.

To justify these results, we proceed in two main steps. In the first step, we derive the point-interaction approximation (also called the Foldy-Lax approximation) of the fields generated by the all-dielectric nanoparticles. Therefore, we reduce the Lippmann-Schwinger equations (L.S.E) stated on the union of the nanoparticles by a linear algebraic system having as a matrix, to invert, defined with the dyadic fundamental solution taken on the locating centers of these nanoparticles. The justification of this step, taking into account the whole cluster of nanoparticles and their high contrasting values of the indices of refraction, is highly non trivial. These approximations, in those regimes, are secured in
[
15
]
. The second step, which is the object of the current work, aims at deriving the limit of this linear algebraic system to an eventual system of integral equations which will play the role of the L.S.E modeling the wave propagation of the effective electromagnetic field (with the generated effective permittivity/permeability). Characterizing this limiting problem is also highly non trivial. In addition to the technicalities coming from the Maxwell system, other difficulties come from the resonant regimes we are handling. For instance, a key step in the analysis is to derive the well-posedness and smoothing property for the effective Maxwell system. In regimes where the effective medium is positive, this smoothing property is derived by restating the corresponding L.S.E as
Id+K
where
Id
is the identity operator and
K
is ’smaller’ in related Hölder spaces where the unknowns are the electromagnetic fields inside the domain and their normal traces on the boundary, see
[
16
]
. In the case where the effective medium is negative, we rewrite the corresponding L.S.E as
Inv+K
where
Inv:=Id-M
with
Id
is the identity operator and
M
is related to the Magnetization operator, here
K
is ’relatively smaller’ as compared
Inv
in some regimes. The operator
Inv
can be inverted, with the needed control of its lower bound, using the spectral decomposition of the Magnetization operator projected on the three subspaces splitting the energy space via the Helmholtz decomposition:
\mathbb{L}^{2}=\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)\overset{\perp}{\otimes}\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)\overset{\perp}{\otimes}\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
, see (
1.7
). The control of the lower bounds of
Inv
is key in justifying the point (3) above, precisely to show how the field becomes enhanced and eventually derive giant electromagnetic fields. This property suggest that the Maxwell operator, defined with the mentioned negative permeability, supports low frequency resonances, in the resolvent sense, which are related to the eigenvalues of the Magnetization operator. A rigorous justification of the last assertion can be achieved in the lines of
[
28
]
where the acoustic model was studied and a low frequency resonance, through the Minnaert frequency, of the related Hamiltonian is shown.

In this work, we distribute the all-dielectric nanoparticles in 3D-domains. The proposed approach to analyse the elecromagnetic field can also be applied to distribution in more general manifolds. Of a particular interest to us are 2D-type nanostructures where, we distribute the nanoparticles in superpositions of 2D-surfaces (flat or not). The following two situations are of special importance:

(1)

Moiré metamaterials
,
[
35
]
. Briefly, the
3
D Moiré metamaterials are built as superpositions and ’rotations’ of surfaces while Moiré metasurfaces are build as superpositions and ’rotations’ of lines. As, we can generate
2
D and 1
D
blocks (i.e. metasurfaces and metawires), we do believe that we can provide with rigorous mathematical models for the Moiré metamaterials or metasurfaces.

(2)

van der Waals heterostructures
,
[
31
]
. The van der Waals heterostructures are built as superposition of 2D-sheets, of subwavelenght nanoparticles, which are globally periodic but locally formed of heterogeneous clusters of nanoparticles. Example of such distributions as trigonal prismatic, octahidral, chalcogenides, Boron nitrids, etc. see
[
31
,
18
]
.

Event though these phenomena are reported with the modeling at the quantum scale, we are tempted to analyze this challenging issue, first based on the Maxwell models, which is known to be a good model at scales of tens to hundreds of nanometers.

1.2.
The electromagnetic fields generated by a cluster of all-dielectric nano-particles.

We denote by
D:=\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\,D_{m}
a collection of
\aleph
connected, bounded and
C^{1}
–smooth particles of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Consider the electromagnetic scattering of time-harmonic electromagnetic plane waves, which can be described by the following Maxwell system

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{Curl}\,E^{T}-ik\mu_{r}H^{T}=0\quad\mbox{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\mathrm{Curl}\,H^{T}+ik\epsilon_{r}E^{T}=0\quad\mbox{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{3},\\
E^{T}=E^{in}+E^{s},\quad H^{T}=H^{in}+H^{s},\\
\sqrt{\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}^{-1}}H^{s}\times\frac{x}{|x|}-E^{s}={\mathcal{O}}({\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}}),\quad\mbox{as}\ |x|\rightarrow\infty,\end{cases}

where
\epsilon_{0}
and
\mu_{0}
are respectively the electric permittivity and the magnetic permeability of the vacuum outside
D
,
\epsilon_{r}\,:=\,\dfrac{\epsilon}{\epsilon_{0}}
and
\mu_{r}\,:=\,\dfrac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}
are the relative permittivity and permeability fulfilling that
\epsilon_{r}=1
outside
D
while
\mu_{r}=1
in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Taking the incident plane wave

E^{Inc}(x)\,=\,\mathrm{p}\,e^{i\,k\,\theta\cdot x}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{Inc}(x)\,=\,\left(\theta\times\mathrm{p}\right)\,e^{i\,k\,\theta\cdot x},

satisfying

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{Curl}\left(E^{Inc}\right)\,-\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}\,=\,0,\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\\
\mathrm{Curl}\left(H^{Inc}\right)\,+\,i\,k\,E^{Inc}=0,\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{cases}

with
\theta,\mathrm{p}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, such that
\mathrm{p}\cdot\theta\,=\,0
, where
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the unit sphere, as a direction of incidence, then this problem is well-posed in appropriate Sobolev spaces, see
[
17
,
30
]
, and the scattered wave
(E^{s},H^{s})
possesses the coming behaviors

(1.3)

E^{s}(x)=\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)+O(|x|^{-1})\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

and

H^{s}(x)=\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(H^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)+O(|x|^{-1})\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

where
(E^{\infty}(\hat{x},,\theta,p),H^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p))
is the corresponding electromagnetic far-field pattern of (
1.1
) in the propagation direction
\hat{x}\,:=\,\dfrac{x}{|x|}
. Suppose that

(1.4)

D_{m}\,=\,a\,{B}_{m}\,+\,{z}_{m},\,m=1,\cdots,\aleph,

where
D_{m}
are the
\aleph
connected small components of the medium
D
, which are characterized by the parameter
a>0
and the locations
{z}_{m}
. Each
{B}_{m}
containing the origin is a bounded Lipschitz domain and fulfills that
{B}_{m}\subset B(0,1)
, where
B(0,1)
is the ball centered at the origin with radius 1. We denote

(1.5)

a:=\max\limits_{1\leq m\leq\aleph}\mathrm{diam}(D_{m})\quad\mbox{and}\quad d:=\min\limits_{{1\leq m,j\leq\aleph}\atop{m\neq j}}d_{mj}:=\min\limits_{{1\leq m,j\leq\aleph}\atop{m\neq j}}\mathrm{dist}(D_{m},D_{j}).

On the basis of the Foldy-Lax approximation presented in
[
15
]
, we investigate the corresponding effective permittivity and permeability generated by the cluster of nano-particles with high relative permittivity contrast under the following assumptions.

Assumptions
.

I).

Assumption on the shape of
B_{m}
.
Assume that the shapes of
B_{m}
’s introduced in
(
1.4
)
are the same and we denote

(1.6)

B:=B_{m}\quad\mbox{for}\quad m=1,\cdots,\aleph.

The domain
B
is a bounded and
C^{1}
-smooth domain that contains the origin. Recall the Helmholtz decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
space, for any bounded domain
B
,

(1.7)

\mathbb{L}^{2}=\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)\overset{\perp}{\otimes}\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)\overset{\perp}{\otimes}\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic.

Then, the projection of an arbitrary vector field
F
onto three subspaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0),\;\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
can be respectively represented as
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(F\right),\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(F\right)
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(F\right)
. We define the vector Newtonian potential operator
{\bf N}\,:=\,{\bf N}_{B}
for any vector function
F
as

(1.8)

{\bf N}(F)(x)\,:=\,\int_{B}\Phi_{0}(x,z)\,F(z)\,dz,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\Phi_{0}(x,z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{1}{|x-z|}\quad(x\neq z).

For the Newtonian operator, under the previous decomposition, we denote
\left(\lambda_{n}^{(2)}(B),e_{n}^{(2)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as the related eigen-system (of its projection) over the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)
, and we denote
\left(\lambda_{n}^{(1)}(B),e_{n}^{(1)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as the related eigen-system (of its projection) over the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
. Since

\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\equiv Curl\left(\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl\right)\cap\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\right),

see for instance
[
7
]
, then for any
n
, there exists
\phi_{n}\in\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl)\cap\mathbb{H}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
, such that

(1.9)

e_{n}^{(1)}=Curl(\phi_{n})\,\,\mbox{ with }\,\,\nu\times\phi_{n}=0\,\,\text{and}\,\,\mathrm{div}\,(\phi_{n})=0.

We assume that, for a certain
n_{0}
,

(1.10)

\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0}}(y)\,dy\,\neq\,0,

where
\phi_{n_{0}}
fulfills
(
1.9
)
with
n=n_{0}
. Then we can define a constant tensor
{\bf P_{0}}
by

(1.11)

{\bf{P}}_{0}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{\lambda_{n_{0}}}}\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0},\ell}(y)\,dy\otimes\overline{\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0},\ell}(y)\,dy},\quad\ell_{\lambda_{n_{0}}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\star},

with
\phi_{n_{0},\ell}
fulfilling

e^{(1)}_{n_{0},\ell}=Curl(\phi_{n_{0},\ell}),\,\mathrm{div}\,(\phi_{n_{0},\ell})=0,\,\nu\times\phi_{n_{0},\ell}=0,\mbox{ and }{\bf N}(e^{(1)}_{n_{0},\ell})=\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)e^{(1)}_{n_{0},\ell}.

II).

Assumptions on the permittivity and permeability of each particle. In order to investigate the electromagnetic scattering by all-dielectric nano-particles with high contrast electric permittivity parameter, we assume that for a positive constant
\eta_{0}
, independent of
a
,

(1.12)

\eta:=\epsilon_{r}-1=\eta_{0}\;a^{-2},\;\mbox{ with }\;\;\;~~a\ll 1,

and the relative magnetic permeability
\mu_{r}
to be moderate, namely
\mu_{r}=1
.

III).

Assumption on the used incident frequency
k
. There exists a positive constant
c_{0}
, independent of
a
, such that

(1.13)

1\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)\,=\,\pm\;c_{0}\;a^{h},\;\;a\ll 1,

where
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)
is the eigenvalue corresponding to
e_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
in
(\ref{pre-cond})
with
n=n_{0}
.

IV).

Assumptions on the distribution of the cluster of particles. Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain of unit volume, containing the particles
D_{m}
,
m=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
. We divide
\Omega
into
[d^{-3}]
periodically distributed subdomains
\Omega_{m}
,
m=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, such that
z_{m}\in D_{m}\subset\Omega_{m}
with
z_{m}
centering at
\Omega_{m}
.
Then we see that
\aleph={\mathcal{O}}(d^{-3})
. Let each
\Omega_{m}
be a cube of volume
d^{3}
.
To further describe the relation between
d
and
a
, we take a positive constant
c_{r}
to be the dilution parameter such that

(1.14)

{a^{3-h}}=c_{r}^{-3}{d^{3}}.

Figure 1
.
A schematic illustration for a periodic distribution of all-dielectric nano-particles.

The above schematization describes a way of subdividing the domain
\Omega
, see Figure
1
.

Remark 1.1
.

The following remarks are necessary regarding these assumptions.

(1)

An extra condition on
\eta_{0}
in
(
1.12
)
to be small enough or
c_{r}
in
(
1.14
)
to be large enough has to be proposed if we distribute the maximum numbers of particles, i.e.
\aleph\sim d^{-3}
.

(2)

Since the shape of
\Omega
is arbitrary, the intersecting part between the set of the cubes and
\partial\Omega
is not necessarily to be empty, unless
\Omega
is a cube. As the volume of
\Omega_{m}
satisfies
|\Omega_{m}|={\mathcal{O}}(d^{3})
, then the maximum radius of
\Omega_{m}
is of order
d
. Thus the intersecting surfaces, of the eventual
\Omega_{m}
’s, with
\partial\Omega
possess the area of order
d^{2}
. Since the total area of
\partial\Omega
is of order one, we deduce that the number of such particles near
\partial\Omega
will not exceed the order of
d^{-2}
, and therefore the volume of this set is of order
d
under the regime of
(
1.14
)
, as
a\rightarrow 0
.

(3)

Among all the available decompositions of the energy space
L^{2}(\Omega)
, the one given by
(\ref{hel-decomp})
is natural as we know that for the Newtonian operator, when restricted to
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
and/or
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
admits an eigen-system and when restricted to
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
it can be neglected, since its presence in the L.S.E is multiplied by the frequency
k^{2}
which we consider as a small parameter. Similarly, for the Magnetization operator, when restricted to
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
is a vanishing one, when restricted to
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
it will be reduced to the identity operator and, finally, when we restrict it to
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
subspace admits an eigen-system.

(4)

Equation
(\ref{condition-on-k})
’s sign selection is dependent on how the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)
is approached (i.e. from left or right). Consequently, the obtained results will be given in term of the selected sign.

Our starting point is the following proposition.

Proposition 1.2
.

[
15
, Theorem 1.3]

Consider the electromagnetic scattering problem
(
1.1
)
generated by a cluster of nano-particles
D_{1},\cdots,D_{\aleph}
. Under
Assumptions
, for
\frac{9}{11}<h<1
, the electric far-field of the scattered wave possesses the expansion as

(1.15)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)=-\,\frac{i\,k^{3}\,\eta}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times Q_{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

uniformly in all directions
\hat{x}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}^{2}
, where

\left(Q_{m}\right)_{m=1,\cdots,\aleph}
is the vector solution to the following algebraic system

(1.16)

Q_{m}-\frac{\eta\,k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot{Q}_{j}=\frac{i\,k}{\pm c_{0}}\,a^{5-h}\;{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{m}),

with
\Upsilon_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the dyadic Green’s function given by

(1.17)

\Upsilon_{k}(x,z):=\underset{x}{\nabla}\,\underset{x}{\nabla}\,\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,+\,k^{2}\,\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,{\bf I},\quad\text{for}\quad x\neq z,

where

(1.18)

\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,:=\,\dfrac{e^{i\,k\,\left|x-z\right|}}{4\,\pi\,\left|x-z\right|},\quad\text{for}\quad x\neq z,

being the fundamental solution to the Helmholtz equation, and
{\bf{P}}_{0}
is the polarization tensor introduced in
(
1.11
)
. In particular,
(
1.16
)
is invertible under the condition

(1.19)

\frac{k^{2}|\eta|a^{5}}{d^{3}\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\right|}\left|{\bf{P}}_{0}\right|<1.

Remark 1.3
.

In
Proposition
1.2
, the condition
(\ref{condi-inver-p1})
is just a sufficient condition to ensure the invertibility of the algebraic system
(
1.16
)
. However, if we take
-c_{0}
in the denominator of
(
1.16
)
, then the condition
(
1.19
)
can be released, see
Remark
1.5
.

1.3.
The effective medium and the generated fields

On the basis of
Assumptions
as well as the Foldy-Lax approximation presented in
Proposition
1.2
, we shall investigate the corresponding effective medium generated by the cluster of all-dielectric nano-particles. We show in this current work that even if the dielectric nano-particles are merely generated by the contrasts of their permittivity, the effective medium is a perturbation of the permeability and not the permittivity. We denote
\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}
and
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
as the corresponding effective relative permittivity and permeability, respectively. We state the main results by distinguishing two cases, one related to the positive definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
and the second to the negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain with
C^{1,\alpha}
-regularity, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then under
Assumptions
, there holds the following expansion for the far-fields

(1)

For positive definite
\mathring{\mu}_{r}
.

There exists a positive constant
c_{\mathrm{reg}}(\alpha,\Omega)
, depending only on
\alpha
and
\Omega
, such that if

\left(k^{3+\alpha}+k^{3}+k^{2}+k+1\right)\;c_{\mathrm{reg}}(\alpha,\Omega)\;c_{r}^{-3}\;\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\;<\;1,

then

\displaystyle E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\hat{x},\theta,p)-E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{k^{5}\,\eta_{0}^{2}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert^{2}}{c_{0}\,\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|

(1.20)

\displaystyle\cdot\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,c_{r}^{2\,\alpha}\,a^{2\,\alpha\,(1-\frac{h}{3})}\,\left|\log(a)\right|^{2}+\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,c_{r}^{\frac{12}{7}}\,a^{\frac{12}{7}(1-\frac{h}{3})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

uniformly in all directions
\hat{x}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}^{2}
, where

E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\cdot)
is the far-field pattern in the same sense as
(
1.3
)
, associated with the following electromagnetic scattering problem for the effective medium as
a\rightarrow 0
,

(1.21)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{curl}E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}-ik{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}=0,\quad\mathrm{curl}H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}+ik{\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}}E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}=0\quad\mbox{in}\ \Omega,\\
\mathrm{curl}E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}-ikH^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}=0,\quad\mathrm{curl}H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}+ikE^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}=0\quad\mbox{in}\ \mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\Omega,\\
E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}=E_{s}^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}+E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}_{in},\quad H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}=H_{s}^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}+H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}_{in},\\
\nu\times E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}|_{+}=\nu\times E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}|_{-},\quad\nu\times H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}|_{+}=\nu\times H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}|_{-}\quad\mbox{on}\ \partial\Omega,\\
H^{{\mathring{\mu}}_{r}}_{s}\times\frac{x}{|x|}-E^{{\mathring{\epsilon}}_{r}}_{s}={\mathcal{O}}({\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}}),\quad\mbox{as}\ |x|\rightarrow\infty\end{cases}

and
[H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}
denotes the Hölder norm of
H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
. The effective permittivity and permeability are respectively given by

(1.22)

\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}={\bf I}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\mu}_{r}={\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}\left({\bf I}-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}{\bf P_{0}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega.

(2)

For negative definite
\mathring{\mu}_{r}
.

Let the domain
B
, introduced in
(
1.6
)
, be the unit ball
B(0,1)
.
1
1

1

The reason why we assume that
B
is a ball is that, in this case, the tensor
{\bf P_{0}}
becomes isotropic (proportional to the identity matrix). Handling the plasmonic resonances with anisotropic permeability (or permittivity) is quite challenging but very interesting. This general case needs certainly to be studied with a different approach than the one we propose in
Section
3
.
Under the condition
k^{2}>\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}\,\dfrac{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}{\eta_{0}}
,

(1.23)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},-}(\hat{x},\theta,p)-E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\Bigg(\frac{k^{5}\eta_{0}^{2}c_{r}^{-3}}{c_{0}\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,\pi^{3}\,-12\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\right)}\left|\left(1\,\mp\,\frac{4\,\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pi^{3}\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\right)^{-1}\right|

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,c_{r}^{2\,\alpha}\,a^{2\,\alpha\,\left(1-\frac{h}{3}\right)}\,\left|\log\left(a\right)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,c_{r}^{\frac{12}{7}}\,a^{\frac{12}{7}\left(1-\frac{h}{3}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

uniformly in all directions
\hat{x}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
where
[H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}
is the Hölder coefficient.
E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},-}(\cdot)
is the far-field associated with the electromagnetic scattering problem
(
1.21
)
where the effective permittivity and permeability are respectively given by

(1.24)

\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}={\bf I}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\mu}_{r}\,=\,\frac{\pi^{3}\,\pm 8\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}}{\pi^{3}\,\mp 4\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}}\,{\bf I},\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega.

The justification of
Theorem
1.4
highly relies on the
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity of the solution
(E^{\mathring{\epsilon_{r}}},H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}})
to (
1.21
), which has been investigated in
[
16
, Theorem 2.2]
for positive definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
. For
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite,
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity of the solution also plays a key role in deriving the corresponding effective medium and we shall later give a separate proposition to guarantee it. The following theorem presents the other main result of the effective medium generated by the cluster of all-dielectric nano-particles with negative definite effective permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
. Before presenting the main theorem with respect to negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, we first introduce some notations. Denote

(1.25)

\xi:=\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}},

where
\eta_{0}
and
c_{0}
are given in (
1.12
) and (
1.13
), respectively. We also define for any vector function
F\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)
, the Magnetization operator
\nabla{\bf M}
as

(1.26)

\nabla{\bf M}_{D}(F)(x):=\underset{x}{\nabla}\int_{D}\underset{z}{\nabla}\Phi_{0}(x,z)\cdot F(z)\,dz,\quad x\in D,

where
\Phi_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)
is given by
(\ref{def-new-operator})
. It is known that the Magnetization operator
\nabla{\bf M}_{D}:\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic\rightarrow\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
is self-adjoint, bounded and its spectrum
\sigma(\nabla{\bf M}_{D})
satisfies
\sigma(\nabla{\bf M}_{D})\subset]0,1]
with
\dfrac{1}{2}
as the only accumulation point of its spectrum. We refer to
[
32
,
19
,
20
,
22
]
and
[
21
]
for more discussions on other properties of
\nabla{\bf M}
.

Remark 1.5
.

Three remarks are in order.

(1)

In the case where the tensor
{\bf P_{0}}
is defined over a ball, and then
{\bf P_{0}}\;=\;\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\;{\bf I}
, see (
3.4
), the condition

\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,<\,\frac{\pi^{3}}{8}

ensures the positive definiteness of the permeability tensor
\mathring{\mu}_{r}
, as it can be seen from (
1.22
) .

(2)

In
Theorem
1.4
, to generate the effective medium with negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, the invertibility condition
(
1.19
)
can be removed. Indeed, for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite, it suffices to satisfy

{\bf I}\,\neq\,\frac{\eta k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\overset{(\ref{exp-p0})}{=}\,\frac{4\,\eta\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}\,\pi^{3}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,{\bf I},

which is obviously fulfilled, since we have the freedom to choose the parameters
\eta
,
k
,
c_{0}
and
c_{r}
, which appear on the right hand side (R.H.S) of the previous in-equation.

(3)

With
(
1.25
)
and
(
1.24
)
,
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
can be represented as

\mathring{\mu_{r}}=\left(1\pm\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\xi\left(1\mp\frac{4}{\pi^{3}}\xi\right)^{-1}\right){\bf I}.

It is easy to verify that for
\xi\,>\,\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}
,

1\pm\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\xi\left(1\mp\frac{4}{\pi^{3}}\xi\right)^{-1}<0,

which indicates that
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
is negative definite.
Theorem
1.4
presents the effective medium associated with positive or negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
. In both cases, and with the stronger condition that
B
is a unit ball for the case of negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, the
C^{0,\alpha}
regularity of the solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem
(
1.21
)
is obtained. Under this Hölder regularity result, the error term
(
1.23
)
can be regarded as a particular form of the error
(
1
)
in
Theorem
1.4
by taking the explicit expression of the constant tensor
{\bf P_{0}}
for negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, see
Section
3
for more detailed discussions.

1.4.
Extended quasi-static plasmonics and the generated giant electromagnetic fields

The interesting consequence of this last result is that for the effective permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative, we can generate plasmonic resonators under quasi-static regime from the cluster of all-dielectric sub-wavelength resonators. We present this result in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.6
.

We follow the same notations and conditions in
Theorem
1.4
,
(2)
. In addition, we assume
\Omega
to satisfy the following upper bound for its surface area to volume ratio

(1.27)

\frac{|\partial\Omega|}{8\;|\Omega|}<1.

Then we have the following results.

(1)

Let
\Omega
be a
C^{4,\alpha}
-smooth domain
2
2

2

This extra regularity condition is needed in the proof of
Proposition
3.3
.
and
\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega)\,>\,\dfrac{1}{3}
be any eigenvalue of the Magnetization operator
\nabla{\bf M}
, defined in
\Omega
. We choose the incident frequency
k
of the form

(1.28)

k^{2}=\frac{1}{\eta_{0}\;\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)}\,+\,\beta\,\frac{\left(3\,\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega)\,-\,1\right)}{\eta_{0}\;\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B)},

where
\beta
is a small parameter, such that
k^{2}\,\leq\,C^{\text{te}}\,\left|\beta\right|
, with
C^{\text{te}}
as a constant independent on
k
and
\beta
. Then, in the regime where
\eta_{0}\gg 1
and under the assumption of the existence of
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{m_{0}}(\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega),\beta)\neq 0,

the far-field
E^{\infty}(\cdot)
admits the following approximation

(1.29)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)=\frac{\pm\,3\,k^{2}}{\left|\beta\right|\;\left(3\,\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}\left(\Omega\right)-1\right)^{2}}\,\hat{x}\,\times\,\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{m_{0}}(\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega),\beta)\cdot\left(\theta\,\times\,\mathrm{p}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(k^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{k^{3}}{\left|\beta\right|}\right),

uniformly in all directions
\hat{x}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}^{2}
, where the tensor
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{m_{0}}(\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega),\beta)
is given by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{X}}_{m_{0}}(\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega),\beta):=\int_{\Omega}e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(z)\;dz\otimes\int_{\Omega}\overline{\LARGE\textbf{N}^{-1}\left(e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}\right)}(x)\,dx.

with

\LARGE\textbf{N}:=\left({\bf I}\,-\,\dfrac{\pm\,k^{2}}{2\,\left|\beta\right|\,\left(3\,\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega)\,-\,1\right)^{2}}\left(N+N^{\prime}\right)\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\,\right)

where
N(\cdot)
is the Newtonian operator given by
(\ref{def-new-operator})
,
N^{\prime}(\cdot)
is the operator defined by

(1.30)

N^{\prime}(F)(x)\,:=\,\int_{\Omega}\,\Phi_{0}(x,z)\,\frac{(x-z)\otimes(x-z)}{\lVert x-z\rVert^{2}}\cdot F(z)\,dz,\quad x\in\Omega.

(2)

(3)

Let
\Omega
be a ball. In this case, we know that
\lambda_{1}^{(3)}(\Omega)=\dfrac{1}{3}
and
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}e_{1}^{(3)}(z)\;dz\,\neq\,0
, and under the assumption

\langle\left(N\,+\,N^{\prime}\right)(e_{1}^{(3)});e_{1}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\,\neq\,0,

where
N(\cdot)
is the Newtonian operator given by
(\ref{def-new-operator})
and
N^{\prime}(\cdot)
is the operator defined by
(\ref{DefNprimeTHM})
, we have

(1.31)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm\,i\,2\,\pi\,k^{-1}\,\hat{x}\times\left(\theta\times\mathrm{p}\right)\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),

uniformly in all directions
\hat{x}\,\in\,\mathbb{S}^{2}
, where the frequency
k
is taken to be of the form

(1.32)

k^{4}\,=\,\frac{\pi^{3}\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}{6\,\eta_{0}\,\left\langle\left(N+N^{\prime}\right)\left(e_{1}^{(3)}\right);e_{1}^{(3)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}},

with
\eta_{0}\,\gg\,1
(and then
k\ll 1
).

Remark 1.7
.

Regarding
Theorem
1.6
, the following remarks are in order.

(1)

The condition (
1.27
) is not restrictive as it is satisfied by many shapes (as sphere, cube, polyhedron, etc), see
[
38
]
. In addition, taking any of these domains and apply a dilation with a magnitude of order larger than one then we get domains satisfying (
1.27
). Finally, general convex bodies with appropriate affine transformations will also satisfy (
1.27
), see
[
10
]
. Let us mention that the surface area to volume ratio appears in many areas of applied sciences as well.

(2)

For
k
satisfying
(
1.28
)
, with
\eta_{0}\,\gg\,1
and
\beta\,\ll\,1
, we have a giant amplification of the magnetic field
H^{Inc}(\cdot)
. In other words, the incident frequency

k_{n_{0}}\,:=\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{\eta_{0}\;\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\left(B\right)}},

is a low-frequency (or quasi-static) plasmonic resonance generated by the cluster of the all-dielectric nano-particles.
Moreover, the corresponding effective permeability now possesses the form

\mathring{\mu_{r}}\,=\,\left(\,1\,-\,\frac{1}{\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega)}\,+\,\mathrm{T}\left(\beta\right)\right)\;{\bf I}.

with
\left|\mathrm{T}\left(\beta\right)\right|\;\ll\;\left|\beta\right|\ll 1
. Remember that
\lambda^{(3)}_{n}(\Omega)\,<\,1
, for every
n
, hence
\mathring{\mu_{r}}<0
for
|\beta|\ll 1
.

(3)

If we choose the back-scattering direction, i.e.
\hat{x}=-\theta
, then the formula
(\ref{Eq126})
will be reduced to

E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)\,=\,\pm\,i\,2\,\pi\,k^{-1}\,\mathrm{p}\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),

where
k
is given by
(\ref{Ballkscale})
. In particular, the generated electromagnetic field is very large and directed into the polarization direction
p
. In addition, this giant behavior of the electromagnetic field infers that the frequency in
(\ref{Ballkscale})
behaves as a quasi-static Plasmonic resonance.

(4)

In
(\ref{far-E})
, if
\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}e_{n}^{(3)}(z)\,dz=0
,
\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N}
, by expanding the function
e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}
with respect to the frequency
k
, see the far-field expression given by
(\ref{add-coro-E})
, neglecting the zeroth order term, keeping the first order term and repeating the same computations we get the following formula

\displaystyle E^{\infty}\left(\hat{x},\theta,p\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}\,4\,\pi}{9\,\left(3\,\lambda_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(\Omega)\,-\,1\right)}\;\hat{x}\times\left(\theta\times\mathrm{p}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{2\,k^{2}}{\langle\left(N+N^{\prime}\right)\left(e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}\right);e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}}\;\hat{x}\times\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{m_{0}}\left(\hat{x},\theta\right)\,\cdot\,\left(\theta\times\mathrm{p}\right)\right)\;+\;\mathcal{O}\left(k^{3}\right),

where
N(\cdot)
is the Newtonian operator given by
(\ref{def-new-operator})
,
N^{\prime}(\cdot)
is the operator defined by
(\ref{DefNprimeTHM})
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{m_{0}}\left(\hat{x},\theta\right)
is the tensor given by

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{m_{0}}\left(\hat{x},\theta\right):=\int_{\Omega}\hat{x}\cdot z\;e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(z)\,dz\otimes\int_{\Omega}\theta\cdot z\;\overline{e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}}(z)\,dz.

(5)

To avoid complicated computations and presentation, we have focused on the case
\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(\Omega)\geq\dfrac{1}{3}
. To deal with the other eventual case
3
3

3

For a general shape
\Omega
, we have
0\,<\,\lambda^{(3)}_{m}(\Omega)\,<\,1
and for
\Omega
as a ball we have
\frac{1}{3}\,<\,\lambda^{(3)}_{m}(\Omega)\,<\,\frac{2}{3}
. The existence of shapes having such eigenvalues smaller than
\frac{1}{3}
is shown in
[
2
, Theorem 3.4]
for instance.
, i.e.
\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(\Omega)<\dfrac{1}{3}
, we need to look for
\xi
as the solution to the full dispersion equation

\left(\pi^{3}\,+\,4\,\xi\right)\,-\,12\,\xi\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(\Omega)\,+\,12\,\xi\,\langle K(e_{n}^{(3)});e_{n}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\;=\;0,

which by using
(\ref{ANC})
becomes

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{4}\,+\,\pi\,4\,\xi\,\left(1-\,3\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(\Omega)\right)\,+\pi\,6\,\xi k^{2}\,\langle\left(N+N^{\prime}\right)(e_{n}^{(3)});e_{n}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+2\,\xi\,i\,k^{3}\,\left|\int_{\Omega}e_{n}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\right|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 3\,\xi\,\sum_{j\geq 3}\,\frac{(i\,k)^{j+1}}{(j+1)!}\,\langle\int_{\Omega}\nabla\nabla\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}\cdot e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy;e_{n}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 3\,\xi\,k^{2}\,\sum_{j\geq 1}\,\frac{(i\,k)^{j+1}}{(j+1)!}\,\langle\int_{\Omega}\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}\,e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy;e_{n}^{(3)}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

which can be seen, recalling that
\xi=\dfrac{k^{2}\,\eta_{0}}{c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}
, see
(\ref{def-xi})
, as a polynomial function in terms of the frequency
k
. With similar arguments, we derive the same results as in
Theorem
1.6
, for
\lambda^{(3)}_{m}(\Omega)\,<\,\dfrac{1}{3}
.

Remark 1.8
.

Thanks to
[
2
, Theorem 3.4]
, we know that for any
l\,\in\,]0,1]
, there exists a smooth surface
\partial\Omega^{l}
, such that
l
is an eigenvalue of
\nabla{\bf M}
, in
\Omega^{l}
. In particular, for any
\varsigma\,\ll\,1
, there existences
\Omega^{\pm\,\varsigma}
such that
\dfrac{1}{3}\,\pm\,\varsigma\,\in\,\sigma\left(\nabla{\bf M}_{\Omega^{\pm\,\varsigma}}\right)
, and in this case
(\ref{far-E})
takes the following form

E^{\infty}(\hat{x},\theta,p)=\frac{-\,6}{\varsigma^{4}}\,\hat{x}\,\times\,\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Z}}_{m_{0}}\cdot\left(\mathrm{p}\times\theta\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varsigma^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(k\right),

with

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Z}}_{m_{0}}\,:=\,\int_{\Omega}e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}(z)\;dz\,\otimes\,\int_{\Omega}\,\overline{\left(\left(N+N^{\prime}\right)\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\,\right)^{-1}\left(e_{m_{0}}^{(3)}\right)}\,dx.

Hence, as
\varsigma
is taken to be small, we observe a huge amplification of the electric field.

Let us finish this part of the section by stressing that although the results presented in
Theorem
1.4
and
Theorem
1.6
are derived for the farfields
E^{\infty}\left(\hat{x},\theta,p\right)
,
\hat{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, they can be also derived, in a similar way, for the scattered fields
E^{s}\left(x,\theta,p\right)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
.

1.5.
Formal derivation of the effective medium

Based on
Proposition
1.2
, formula
(\ref{linear-discrete})
, we have the following linear algebraic system

\displaystyle U_{m}\,-\,\frac{\eta\,k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}\,\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{j}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(z_{m})

(1.33)

\displaystyle U_{m}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}\,\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{j}\,

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{contrast-epsilon})}{=}

\displaystyle\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(z_{m}),

where

U_{m}\;:=\;\pm\,c_{0}\,a^{h-5}\,{\bf P_{0}^{-1}}\cdot Q_{m},\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq m\leq\aleph.

Accordingly, the discrete form of the Foldy-Lax approximation given in
Proposition
1.2
, formula
(\ref{approximation-E})
, becomes

(1.34)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{i\,k^{3}\,\eta}{\pm\,4\,\pi\,c_{0}}\,a^{5-h}\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{m}\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}(a^{\frac{h}{3}})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{contrast-epsilon})}{=}

\displaystyle\,-\frac{i\,k^{3}\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,4\,\pi\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{m}\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}(a^{\frac{h}{3}})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{def-xi})}{=}

\displaystyle\,-\frac{i\,k\,\xi}{\pm\,4\,\pi}\,c_{r}^{3}\,a^{3-h}\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{m}\,+\,{\mathcal{O}}(a^{\frac{h}{3}}).

Now, inspired by (
1.33
), we have the following integral form for the magnetic field

(1.35)

H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(x)\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\,=\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in\Omega_{m},

with
\Upsilon_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the dyadic Green’s function given by
(\ref{dyadic-Green})
. Moreover, under Assumption IV, we know that the dilution parameter
c_{r}
fulfills

(1.36)

c_{r}^{-3}\,=\,\frac{a^{3-h}}{d^{3}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\Omega_{m}\right|\,=\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(d^{3}\right)\,=\,{\mathcal{O}}\left(c_{r}^{3}a^{3-h}\right).

This allows us to rewrite
(\ref{PRP1})
as

H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(x)\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,d^{3}\,\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\,=\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in\Omega_{m},

or,

\displaystyle H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(x)\,

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,d^{3}\,\int_{\Omega_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,d^{3}\,\int_{\Omega}\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\,=\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in\Omega_{m}.

Then, from the second term on the left hand side (L.H.S), we extract a constant tensor, that we denote by
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}
, related to the periodic distribution of the cluster of nano-particles, we will provide later with further details, and this can be used to derive the coming Lipmann-Schwinger system of Equation (L.S.E)

(1.37)

H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(x)\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,\left[-\,\nabla{\bf M}^{k}\left({\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)(x)\,+\,k^{2}\,{\bf N^{k}}\left({\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)(x)\right]\,=\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(x,\theta),\quad x\in\Omega,

where
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}
is a constant tensor defined by

(1.38)

{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\,=\,\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}{\bf P_{0}},

with
{\bf P_{0}}
given by (
1.11
),
\nabla{\bf M}^{k}
is the Magnetization operator with non vanishing frequency defined by

\nabla{\bf M}^{k}\left(E\right)(x)\,:=\,\nabla\int_{\Omega}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\cdot E(y)\,dy,\quad x\in\Omega,

and
{\bf N}^{k}
is the Newtonian operator with non vanishing frequency given by

{\bf N^{k}}\left(E\right)(x)\,:=\,\int_{\Omega}\Phi_{k}(x,y)E(y)\,dy,\quad x\in\Omega,

where
\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined by
(\ref{DefFSHE})
. Furthermore, it is straightforward to verify that
H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(\cdot)
in (
1.37
) is the solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem for the effective medium (
1.21
)
with

(1.39)

\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}={\bf I}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathring{\mu}_{r}={\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}},\quad\text{in}\;\;\Omega.

Remark 1.9
.

We add the following two observations.

(1)

By choosing appropriate parameters
c_{0},\eta_{0}
and
c_{r}
, the invertibility of the matrix

(1.40)

{\bf I}-\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}

in
(\ref{exp-T})
can be guaranteed. Indeed, it suffices to let
c_{r}
to be large enough. If, in particular,
{\bf P_{0}}
is constructed for the unit ball
\Omega:=B(0;1)
, then with the explicit form of
{\bf P_{0}}
given by
(\ref{exp-p0})
, it is sufficient to fulfill
\dfrac{4\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}\pi^{3}}c_{r}^{-3}\neq 1
to get the invertibility of (
1.40
).

(2)

By rearranging terms in
(
1.38
)
, we see that
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}
satisfies the following equation

{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\left({\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)^{-1}={\bf P_{0}},

where the invertibility of the matrix
{\bf I}+\dfrac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
can be fulfilled for
c_{r}
large enough, see the observation (1) above as well.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
is devoted to prove
Theorem
1.4
for positive definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
by investigating the effective medium and evaluating the error estimate between the far-fields
E^{\infty}(\cdot)
and
E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\cdot)
, under some necessary a-priori estimates. In
Section
3
, we first present a systematical analysis on the behaviours of
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
by different choices of the parameter
c_{0}
, in particular, the case for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite is studied in detail under certain geometrical setup. Then the
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity of the solution
(E^{\mathring{\epsilon_{r}}}(\cdot),H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}(\cdot))
for negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
is presented and the corresponding proof is given in
Section
4
. Based on the similar a-priori estimates in
Section
2
, we can prove
Theorem
1.4
for negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, accordingly. Finally, with appropriate choice of the parameter
\xi
, we shall generate the plasmonic resonance under quasi-static regime from the cluster of all-dielectric nanoparticles by proving
Theorem
1.6
.
Section
4
is devoted to provide the detailed proof of the a-priori estimates in
Section
2
and the
C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
-regularity of
H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
in
Section
3
.

In the rest of the manuscript, the dependency of the electromagnetic fields
(E,H)
and
(E^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}},H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}})
, with the incident fields, in terms of the incident direction
\theta
and polarization direction
\mathrm{p}
will be omitted to reduce heavy notations.

2.
The effective medium with
\mathring{\mu}_{r}
-Dielectric

In this section, we consider the electromagnetic scattering for the effective medium with positive permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, i.e.
\mathring{\mu_{r}}\,>\,0
. The analyses will be divided into the following three parts. First, we present the Hölder regularity of the solution to the L.S.E (
1.37
), which also indicates the well-posedness for the scattering problem (
1.21
) for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being positive. Then, we give several significant a-priori estimates associated with the L.S.E as well as the discrete linear algebraic system (
1.16
). Finally, we shall prove our main results in
Theorem
1.4
.

2.1.
\boldsymbol{C^{0,\alpha}}
-regularity for the solution
\boldsymbol{H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}}
with
\boldsymbol{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
-Dielectric.

From
[
16
]
, where the effective permittivity and permeability generated by a cluster of moderately contrasting nano-particles are studied, by following a similar argument, we know that for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being positive definite in the scattering problem (
1.21
), under certain conditions with respect to the frequency
k
, the dilution parameter
c_{r}
and the constant tensor
{\bf P_{0}}
, there also holds the Hölder regularity, up to the boundary, of the electromagnetic fields associated with (
1.21
).

Proposition 2.1
.

[
16
, Theorem 2.2]

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain with
C^{1,\alpha}
-regularity, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Regarding the electromagnetic scattering problem
(
1.21
)
, with

\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being positive definite, there exists a positive constant
c_{\mathrm{reg}}(\alpha,\Omega)
, depending only on
\alpha
and
\Omega
, such that if

\left(k^{3+\alpha}+k^{3}+k^{2}+k+1\right)\;c_{\mathrm{reg}}(\alpha,\Omega)\;c_{r}^{-3}\;\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\;<\;1,

then we have

(E^{\mathring{\epsilon}_{r}},H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}})\in C^{0,\alpha}({\overline{\Omega}})\times C^{0,\alpha}({\overline{\Omega}}).

We refer to
[
16
, Theorem 2.2]
for the detailed proof of
Proposition
2.1
. The coming corollary is suggested by the previous proposition.

Corollary 2.2
.

For
1\leq j\leq\aleph
and
z\in\Omega_{j}
, denote
S_{j}\subset\Omega_{j}
as the largest ball contained in
\Omega_{j}
, then we have

(2.1)

\left|H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)-\frac{1}{\left|S_{j}\right|}\,\int_{S_{j}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\right|\;\lesssim\;\left[H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right]_{C^{0,\alpha}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)}\;d^{\alpha}.

2.2.
A-priori estimates with
\boldsymbol{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
-Dielectric.

In order to prove
Theorem
1.4
, we first give some important a-priori estimates related to the L.S.E (
1.37
) and the discrete linear algebraic system (
1.16
) in the following proposition, which is derived by the counting lemmas and the Calderón-Zygmund inequality. Denote
S_{m}\subset\Omega_{m}
as the largest ball contained in
\Omega_{m}
,
m=1,\cdots,\aleph
. Undoubtedly, for every
m
such that
1\leq m\leq\aleph
, we have
\left|S_{m}\right|\sim\left|\Omega_{m}\right|
. Based on
Proposition
2.1
, we have.

Proposition 2.3
.

Under
Assumptions
, there holds the coming algebraic system associated with the L.S.E
(
1.37
)
,

\digamma_{m}\,-\,\pm\,\xi\,d^{3}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}\Upsilon_{k}\left(z_{m},z_{j}\right)\cdot{\bf P}_{0}\cdot\digamma_{j}\,=\,i\,k\,H^{Inc}(z_{m})\,+\,\mathrm{Error}_{m},

where

\digamma_{m}\,:=\,\left({\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(x)\,dx,\quad m\,=\,1,\cdots,\aleph,

with
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}
as the tensor introduced in
(\ref{exp-T})
,
\eta_{0}
is the constant given in
(
1.12
)
and
\mathrm{Error}_{m}
is the corresponding error term satisfying the following estimate

\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\left|\mathrm{Error}_{m}\right|^{2}\lesssim\,\frac{\eta_{0}^{2}k^{4}}{c_{0}^{2}}\,\lvert{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\rvert^{2}\;c_{r}^{-6}\;\left([H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}d^{2\,\alpha\,-\,3}\,\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,d^{-\frac{9}{7}}\right).

In addition, we have

(2.2)

\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\left|\digamma_{m}\,-\,U_{m}\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\lesssim\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}\;\left|{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right|}{\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}\;\left([H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{2\,\alpha-3}\,\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\left\|H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,d^{-\frac{9}{7}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\{U_{m}\}_{1\leq m\leq\aleph}
is the solution to the algebraic system
(\ref{alg-dis-00})
.

In order to clear the structure of our paper, we postpone the proof of
Proposition
2.3
to
Section
4
.

With the regularity results for the solution as well as the necessary a-priori estimates above, we are now in a position to prove
Theorem
1.4
, for the effective medium with
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
-Dielectric as follows.

2.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We start by deriving a useful integral representation of
E^{\infty}_{eff,+}(\cdot)
from
(\ref{LS-1})
. For this, we take the Curl operator in both sides of
(\ref{LS-1})
, to get

\displaystyle Curl\left(H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)(x)\,\mp\,\xi\,k^{2}\,Curl\,\int_{\Omega}\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz\,=\,i\,k\,Curl\left(H^{Inc}\right)(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,E^{Inc}(x)

\displaystyle Curl\left(H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)(x)\,\mp\,\xi\,k^{2}\,\int_{\Omega}\underset{x}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,\times\,{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz\,-\,k^{2}\,E^{T}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,k^{2}\,E^{s}(x).

Since
H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(\cdot)
is the magnetic field generated by a source
i\,k\,H^{Inc}(\cdot)
, instead of
H^{Inc}(\cdot)
, the equations satisfied by
H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(\cdot)
are to understood up to the coefficient
i\,k
, in particular, we have

Curl\left(H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right)\,=\,i\,k\,Curl\left(H^{T}\right)\,=\,-\,i\,k\,i\,k\,E^{T}\,=\,k^{2}\,E^{T},\quad\mbox{for}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Omega}.

Then, we reduce the previous equation to

\,\pm\,\xi\,\int_{\Omega}\underset{x}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,\times\,{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz\,=\,E^{s}(x),\quad\left|x\right|\,\rightarrow\,+\,\infty.

In addition, we have,

\underset{x}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z)\,=\,\frac{i\,k}{4\,\pi}\,\frac{x}{\left|x\right|^{2}}\,e^{i\,k\,\left|x\right|}\,e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left|x-z\right|^{2}}\right),\quad\left|x\right|\,\rightarrow\,+\,\infty.

Then, by plugging this formula into the previous one, we end up with

(2.3)

\frac{e^{i\,k\,\left|x\right|}}{\left|x\right|}\,\left(\,\mp\,\frac{i\,k}{4\,\pi}\,\xi\,\int_{\Omega}\,e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\,\times\,{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left|x\right|}\right)\right)\,=\,E^{s}(x),\quad\left|x\right|\,\rightarrow\,+\,\infty.

Moreover, from
(\ref{far-def})
, we know that

E^{s}(x)\,=\,\frac{e^{i\,k\,\left|x\right|}}{\left|x\right|}\,\left(E^{\infty}_{eff,+}(\hat{x})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left|x\right|}\right)\right),

which, by identification with
(\ref{Eq0224Eq})
, we get the representation

(2.4)

E^{\infty}_{eff,+}(\hat{x})\,=\,\pm\,\frac{i\,k}{4\,\pi}\,\xi\,\int_{\Omega}\,e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\,\times\,{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz.

Next, we use
(\ref{exp-T})
, to rewrite it like

(2.5)

E^{\infty}_{eff,+}(\hat{x})\,=\,\pm\,\frac{i\,k}{4\,\pi}\,\xi\,\int_{\Omega}\,e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz.

By subtracting (
1.34
) from (
2.5
), and using the fact that
\Omega=\left(\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}\right)\cup\left(\Omega\setminus\left(\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}\right)\right)
, we get

(2.6)

E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\hat{x})\,-\,E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\pm\,\frac{i\,k}{4\,\pi}\,\xi\,\left[\,F^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,+\,G^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,\right]+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

where

\displaystyle F^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left(H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)-\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\right)\,dz

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}\left(e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}(y)dy-e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{m}\right)dz,

and

(2.7)

G^{\infty}(\hat{x}):=\int_{\Omega\backslash\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz.

Next, we estimate
\left\|F^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)}
and
\left\|E^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)}
.

(1)

Estimation of
\left\|F^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)}
. To accomplish this, we estimate separately the first and the second term on the R.H.S of
F^{\infty}(\cdot)
.

(a)

Estimation of
F_{1}^{\infty}
.

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left(H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)-\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\right)\,dz

\displaystyle\left|F_{1}^{\infty}(\hat{x})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\left|H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)-\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\right|\,dz

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{AS0456})}{\lesssim}

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\left|\Omega_{m}\right|\,\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\,[H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{\alpha},

where we have used the fact that
{\bf P_{0}}
is a constant tensor. In addition, since
\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\sum}}\left|\Omega_{m}\right|\sim 1
, we obtain

(2.8)

\left\|F_{1}^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\lesssim\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\;\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\,[H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}d^{\alpha}.

(b)

Estimation of
F_{2}^{\infty}
.

F_{2}^{\infty}(\hat{x}):=\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}\left(e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}(y)\,dy-e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot U_{m}\right)\,dz.

By using Taylor expansion near
z_{m}
, we obtain that

e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}=e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}-i\,k\,\int_{0}^{1}(z-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\,e^{-i\,k\,\left(z_{m}+t\,(z-z_{m})\right)}\,dt,\quad z\in\Omega_{m}.

Then,

\displaystyle F_{2}^{\infty}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left[\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy-U_{m}\right]\,dz

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}i\,k\,\int_{0}^{1}(z-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\,e^{-i\,k\,\left(z_{m}+t\,(z-z_{m})\right)}\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\,dz.

We evaluate
F^{\infty}_{2}(\hat{x})
by separately estimating the first and the second term on the R.H.S of the previous equation.

(a)

Estimation of
F_{2,1}^{\infty}
.

\displaystyle F_{2,1}^{\infty}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left[\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy-U_{m}\right]\,dz

\displaystyle\left|F_{2,1}^{\infty}\left(\hat{x}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\Omega_{m}\right|\,\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy-U_{m}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\Omega_{m}\right|\,\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\,\aleph^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy-U_{m}\right|^{2}\,\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

From
(\ref{exp-T})
, we can derive that

\left({\bf I}\,+\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\right)\;=\;\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1},

which will be used together with the estimation
(\ref{es-l2})
, and the fact that
\left|\Omega_{m}\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(d^{3}\right)
and
\aleph=\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-3}\right)
, to deduce the estimation

(2.9)

\left\|F_{2,1}^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\lesssim\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert^{2}\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|}{\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}\;\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{2\,\alpha}\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}\,d^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(b)

Estimation of
F_{2,2}^{\infty}
.

\displaystyle F_{2,2}^{\infty}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}i\,k\,\int_{0}^{1}(z-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\,e^{-i\,k\,\left(z_{m}+t\,(z-z_{m})\right)}\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\hat{x}\times{\bf P_{0}}\cdot\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{|S_{m}|}\int_{S_{m}}H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(y)\,dy\,dz

\displaystyle\left|F_{2,2}^{\infty}\left(\hat{x}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle k\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{\Omega_{m}}\left|z-z_{m}\right|\,dz\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(S_{m}\right)}.

Then,

(2.10)

\left\|F_{2,2}^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\mathcal{O}\left(k\,d\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}\right).

Consequently, by gathering
(\ref{EstF21})
and
(\ref{EstF22})
, we obtain that

(2.11)

\left\|F_{2}^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\lesssim\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert^{2}\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|}{\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}\;\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{2\,\alpha}\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}\,d^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Now, by combining with
(\ref{F1})
and
(\ref{EstF2})
, we obtain,

(2.12)

\left\|F^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\lesssim\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert^{2}\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|}{\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}\;\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{2\,\alpha}\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}\,d^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2)

Estimation of
\left\|G^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(S\right)}
. By taking the modulus on both sides of the equation
(\ref{DefGinf})
, we get:

\displaystyle\left|G^{\infty}(\hat{x})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega\backslash\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z}\,\hat{x}\times\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\cdot{\bf P_{0}}\cdot H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}(z)\,dz\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\Omega\backslash\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}\right|\,\left|{\bf P_{0}}\right|\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\left\|H^{\mathring{\mu}_{r}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

Due to
Remark
1.1
, we know that
\left|\Omega\backslash\underset{m=1}{\overset{\aleph}{\cup}}\Omega_{m}\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(d\right)
, which implies,

(2.13)

\left\|G^{\infty}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lesssim d\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\frac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}.

Now, combining with the estimation of
F^{\infty}(\cdot)
given by
(\ref{EstF})
and the estimation of
G^{\infty}(\cdot)
presented by
(\ref{G})
, under the relation
(\ref{distribute})
, we derive from
(\ref{mid1})
the coming estimation

(2.14)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\hat{x})-E^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{k^{5}\,\eta_{0}^{2}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert^{2}\,\left|\left({\bf I}\,-\,\dfrac{\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pm\,3\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right|}{c_{0}\,\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\,\lvert{\bf P_{0}}\rvert\right)}

\displaystyle\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,c_{r}^{2\,\alpha}\,a^{2\,\alpha\,(1-\frac{h}{3})}\,\left|\log(a)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)}\,c_{r}^{\frac{12}{7}}\,a^{\frac{12}{7}(1-\frac{h}{3})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
. The remaining part of the proof consists in substituting the explicit form of the constant tensor
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
, given by
(\ref{exp-T})
, into
(\ref{effective-permi})
, then derive the expressions of the far-field error given by
(\ref{err-es})
and the effective coefficient as
(\ref{new-coeff})
.

Now we prove the second part of
Theorem
1.4
. Using
(\ref{def-xi})
and substituting
(\ref{exp-T-neg})
into
(\ref{ls-eff})
, the far-field becomes

E_{\mathrm{eff},-}^{\infty}(\hat{x})=\mp\frac{3\,i\,\eta_{0}\,k^{3}}{c_{0}\,\pi^{4}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,\left(1\,\mp\,\frac{4\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pi^{3}\,c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}}\right)^{-1}\int_{\Omega}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z}\hat{x}\times H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}(z)\,dz.

Then, similar to the expression of the error for
E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},+}(\hat{x})-E^{\infty}(\hat{x})
, given by (
2.14
), with the help of the explicit representations of the constant tensors
{\bf P_{0}}
, in
(\ref{exp-p0})
, and
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
, in
(\ref{exp-T-neg})
, we deduce by direct computations that

\displaystyle\left|E^{\infty}_{\mathrm{eff},-}(\hat{x})-E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{k^{5}\,\eta_{0}^{2}\,c_{r}^{-3}}{c_{0}\left(c_{0}\,c_{r}^{3}\,\pi^{3}\,-\,12\,k^{2}\,\eta_{0}\right)}\left|\left(1\mp\frac{4\,\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pi^{3}\,c_{0}}\,c_{r}^{-3}\,\right)^{-1}\right|

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\left([H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}]^{2}_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})}\,d^{2\,\alpha}\,\left|\log(d)\right|^{2}\,+\,\lVert H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,d^{\frac{12}{7}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then (
1.23
) can be obtained directly by using (
1.36
). The negative definite effective permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
is given by the diagonal matrix (
3.5
), while
\mathring{\epsilon_{r}}
keeps unchanged, which proves (
1.24
). This ends the proof of
Theorem
1.4
.

3.
The effective medium with
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
-Plasmonic.

In this section, we investigate the electromagnetic scattering for the effective medium with negative permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
. Precisely, we first present a systematical discussion on the behaviors of the effective permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
appearing in (
1.21
), associated with different values of the parameter
c_{0}
given by (
1.13
), particularly for the case with
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite. Then, similar to the analyses in
Section
2
for positive permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, we study the Hölder regularity of the solution
(E^{\mathring{\epsilon_{r}}},H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}})
to (
1.21
). Indeed, we show that with
\partial\Omega
is
C^{2}-
regular, we obtain the
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity to the solution for negative permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, see
Proposition
3.1
. If in addition
\partial\Omega
is
C^{4,\alpha}-
regular, then we derive
C^{0,\alpha}
-norm estimates of these solutions, see
Lemma
3.3
. Finally, we give the proof of
Theorem
1.4
, point (2), by utilizing the a-priori estimates for the corresponding L.S.E in a similar way as we did in
Proposition
2.3
.

3.1.
Negative definite effective permeability
\boldsymbol{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
.

From the L.S.E (
1.37
), we deduce that the effective permeability is of the form

(3.1)

\mathring{\mu_{r}}={\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\overset{(\ref{exp-T})}{=}{\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}\left({\bf I}-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}{\bf P_{0}},

where
{\bf P_{0}}
is given by
(\ref{defP0})
. In the subsequent discussions, regarding negative contrast permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, we assume that the domain
B
, in
(\ref{defP0})
, is the unit ball
B(0,1)
. In this case, from
[
4
, Section 5.2]
, we know that

(3.2)

\int_{B(0,1)}\phi_{1,j}(x)\,dx\,=\,\frac{4}{\pi}\,\nabla\left(\left|x\right|\,Y_{1}^{j}\left(\hat{x}\right)\right),

where
\phi_{1,j}(\cdot)
is the function defined in
(\ref{Intphin0})
, with
n_{0}=1
, and
Y_{1}^{j}\left(\hat{x}\right)
, with
j=-1,0,1
, are the spherical harmonics given explicitly by

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{-1}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\,\pi}}\,\left(\hat{x}_{1}\,-\,i\,\hat{x}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{0}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\,\hat{x}_{3}

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{1}\left(\hat{x}\right)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2}\,\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\,\pi}}\,\left(\hat{x}_{1}\,+\,i\,\hat{x}_{2}\right).

Using
(\ref{Eq0849})
, we obtain
4
4

4

Consequently,

(3.3)

\int_{B(0,1)}\phi_{1}(x)\,dx\,=\,\frac{2}{\pi}\,\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-i\sqrt{2}\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\phi_{1,-1}(x)\,dx\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\pi}\,\sqrt{\frac{6}{\pi}}\,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-i\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\phi_{1,0}(x)\,dx\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{2}{\pi}\,\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\,\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)}\phi_{1,1}(x)\,dx\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\,\frac{1}{\pi}\,\sqrt{\frac{6}{\pi}}\,\begin{pmatrix}1\\
i\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Now, by using the expression of
{\bf P_{0}}
, given in
(\ref{defP0})
, we obtain

(3.4)

{\bf P_{0}}\;=\;\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\;{\bf I}.

Recall the definition of
\xi
given by
(\ref{def-xi})
. By substituting
(\ref{exp-p0})
and
(\ref{def-xi})
into
(\ref{mu-r0})
, we have

(3.5)

\mathring{\mu_{r}}={\bf I}\pm\xi\left(1\mp\xi\frac{4}{\pi^{3}}\right)^{-1}{\bf I}\cdot\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}{\bf I}\;=\;\frac{\left(\pi^{3}\,\pm\,8\,\xi\right)}{\left(\pi^{3}\,\mp\,4\,\xi\right)}\;{\bf I}.

And, regarding the permeability contrast, we get

\mathring{\mu_{r}}\,-\,{\bf I}\,=\,\frac{\pm\,12\,\xi}{\left(\pi^{3}\,\mp\,4\,\xi\right)}\;{\bf I}.

The above formula, as a function of the chosen sign, suggests two cases

(1)

By taking the lower sign, we obtain

\mathring{\mu_{r}}\,-\,{\bf I}\,=\,\frac{-\,12\,\xi}{\left(\pi^{3}\,+\,4\,\xi\right)}\;{\bf I}\,<\,0.

(2)

By taking the upper sign, we obtain

\mathring{\mu_{r}}\,-\,{\bf I}=\left(\frac{12\,\xi}{\pi^{3}-4\,\xi}\right)\,{\bf I}\,\begin{cases}<0,&\text{\;\; if $\xi>\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}$ \,\, }\\
&\\
>0,&\text{\;\; if $\xi<\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}$ \,\, }\end{cases}.

The above formula, as a functions of the chosen sign, suggests two cases
5
5

5

\mathring{\mu_{r}}
is positive (resp. negative) definite if and only if
\langle x^{T};\mathring{\mu_{r}}\cdot x\rangle\,>\,0
(resp.
\langle x^{T};\mathring{\mu_{r}}\cdot x\rangle\,<\,0
), for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
.

(1)

By taking the lower sign, we obtain

(3.6)

\mathring{\mu_{r}}=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}-8\,\xi}{\pi^{3}+4\,\xi}\right)\,{\bf I}\,\begin{cases}<0,&\text{\;\; if $\xi>\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{8}$}\\
&\\
>0,&\text{\;\; if $0<\xi<\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{8}$}\end{cases}.

(2)

By taking the upper sign, we obtain:

(3.7)

\mathring{\mu_{r}}=\left(\frac{\pi^{3}+8\,\xi}{\pi^{3}-4\,\xi}\right)\,{\bf I}\,\begin{cases}<0,&\text{\;\; if $\xi>\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}$}\\
&\\
>0,&\text{\;\; if $0<\xi<\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}$}\end{cases}.

Therefore, for
\xi:=\dfrac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}>\dfrac{\pi^{3}}{4}
, which means

c_{0}<\dfrac{4\,\eta_{0}\,k^{2}}{\pi^{3}\,c_{r}^{3}},

the tensor
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
is always negative definite. Particularly, if
c_{0}\ll 1
, then in (
3.1
),
\dfrac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}
dominates and there holds

\left({\bf I}-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}=\left(-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\left({\bf I}+\left(-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\right)^{-1}=\left(-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}{\bf P_{0}}\right)^{-1}\left({\bf I}+o(1)\right).

Thus

\mathring{\mu_{r}}={\bf I}+\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}\left(-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{\pm 3c_{0}}c_{r}^{-3}\right)^{-1}{\bf P_{0}}^{-1}{\bf P_{0}}\left({\bf I}+o(1)\right)=-2{\bf I}+o(1),

which indicates that in this case, there will be twice of the enhancement of the negative definite effective permeability. In addition, by the explicit representation of
{\bf P_{0}}
, given by
(\ref{exp-p0})
, we can also derive from the definition of
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
, see for instance
(\ref{exp-T})
, that

(3.8)

{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}\;=\;\left(1\,\mp\,\frac{4}{\pi^{3}}\,\xi\right)^{-1}\,\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\,{\bf I},

with
\xi
as given by
(\ref{def-xi})
.

3.2.
\boldsymbol{C^{0,\alpha}}
-regularity of the solution
\boldsymbol{H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}}
with negative definite
\boldsymbol{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
.

In order to generate the effective medium with negative definite permeability in
Theorem
1.4
, second point, the
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity of the solution
H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
is needed. However, the method utilized in
Proposition
2.1
does not work for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite in the L.S.E (
1.37
). As explained in
Section
1.1
, for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
positive definite, we rewrite the operator appearing in the LSE as
Id+A
where
Id
is the identity operator and
A
is shown to be dominated by
Id
under the conditions in
Proposition
2.1
. In short, in this case we have no plasmonic eigenvalues. However, for
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
negative definite, such plasmon eigenvalues occur, therefore, we rewrite the related operator as
Inv+A
where
Inv
includes the Magnetization operator
\nabla{\bf M}
(which creates the plasmon eigenvalues). Therefore, in this subsection, we prove the
C^{0,\alpha}
-regularity of the solution, in this case, by properly analyzing the operator
Inv
. We state these results in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain which is
C^{2}
- smooth. Regarding the electromagnetic scattering problem
(
1.21
)
, with
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
being negative definite, we have the following regularity result

(E^{\mathring{\epsilon_{r}}},H^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}})\in C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})\times C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega}),

under the condition

2\,\delta\,\pi^{3}\,<\,\xi\,<\,\frac{\pi^{4}}{12\,\left|\Omega\right|\,c(k)},

where
\xi
is the coefficient given by
(\ref{def-xi})
,
c(\cdot)
is the frequency function defined by

(3.9)

c(k):=k^{2}\,+\frac{k^{3}}{6}\,+\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}\,\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{\left(k\,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\right)^{n}}{n!}+\frac{k^{3}}{16}\,\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{\left(k\,\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)\right)^{n}}{n!},

and
\delta
is the parameter given by

\delta:=1+\left[\frac{1}{12\;\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\min}\;\left|\lambda_{n}^{(3)}\left(\Omega\right)-\dfrac{1}{3}\right|}\right],

with the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{n}^{(3)}\left(\Omega\right)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are such that
\lambda_{n}^{(3)}\left(\Omega\right)\,>\,\dfrac{1}{3}
.

We postpone the detailed proof of
Proposition
3.1
to
Section
4
.

3.3.
A-priori estimates with negative definite
\boldsymbol{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
.

The a-priori estimates corresponding to the L.S.E (
1.37
) for negative definite
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
possess the similar expressions to
Proposition
2.3
. Indeed, with the help of the explicit representations of the constant tensors
{\bf P_{0}}
and
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
, which are given by (
3.4
) and (
3.8
), respectively, we can get the corresponding a-priori estimates as follows.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
{\bf T}^{\mathring{\mu_{r}}}
be the constant tensor defined by
(\ref{exp-T})
. For negative definite permeability
\mathring{\mu_{r}}
, under
Assumptions
, we have the following algebraic system associated with the L.S.E
(\ref{LS-1})
,
